\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\odot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(J-(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}}}\right),\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle\,.

let
Y^{(1)}=\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X^{(1)}+E^{(1)}
where
X^{(1)}=Z^{(1)}(Z^{(1)})^{\top}-\frac{1}{k}J
and
E^{(1)}=Y^{(1)}-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X^{(1)}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)}-Y^{(1)}_{S}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Z^{(1)}(Z^{(1)})^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle-\frac{\varepsilon d}{kn}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle J,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle E^{(1)},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle\,.

For the first term, since
\mathbf{0}\leqslant Z^{(1)}(Z^{(1)})^{\top}\leqslant J
and
x^{(1)}\leqslant\mathbf{1}
, it follows that

\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Z^{(1)}(Z^{(1)})^{\top},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle J,sw^{\top}}}\right\rangle=\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}s_{i}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}}}\right)\leqslant 2\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}\lVert w\rVert^{2}\,.

For the second term, since
x^{(1)}\geqslant-\mathbf{1}
, it follows that

-\frac{\varepsilon d}{kn}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle J,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon d}{kn}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle J,sw^{\top}}}\right\rangle=\frac{\varepsilon d}{kn}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}s_{i}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}w_{i}}}\right)\leqslant\frac{2\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}{k}\lVert w\rVert^{2}\,.

For the third term,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{mix}}(Y,Z)
and SoS Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle E^{(1)},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert E^{(1)}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\delta_{\eta}n+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}

Since
\varepsilon^{2}d\gg k^{2}
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\ll\frac{\varepsilon d}{k}
, we can obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle E^{(1)},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot s}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right)^{\top}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.0001\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}{k}n+\frac{0.001\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

Thus, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)}-Y^{(1)}_{S}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.001\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}n\,.

(8.8)

Now, we consider the term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle
. By
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{close}}(Y,\xi;\eta,\bar{G})
, we have

Y^{(1)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}=\bar{G}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}=Y^{(2)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}\,.

By
Lemma

8.4
and
w=v\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)
,

\frac{\mathbf{1}-x^{(1)}}{2}\odot w=\frac{\mathbf{1}+x^{(2)}}{2}\odot w\quad\text{and}\quad x^{(1)}\odot w=-x^{(2)}\odot w\,.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1-x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(2)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(2)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(2)}}{2}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(2)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(2)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(2)}}{2}}}\right\rangle\,.

Consider the first term, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle X^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle E^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle\,.

By
Corollary

8.8
, we have

\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle X^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.008\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

By
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{mix}}(Y,Z)
and SoS Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle E^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\lVert w\rVert^{2}\,.

Since
\varepsilon^{2}d\gg k^{2}
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\ll\frac{\varepsilon d}{k}
, and, therefore,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.01\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

Using similar analysis on the lower bound side, we can obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(2)}\odot w}}\right),x^{(2)}\odot w\odot\frac{1+x^{(2)}}{2}}}\right\rangle\geqslant-\frac{0.005\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

Thus,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}_{S}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.015\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

(8.9)

Plugging
Eq.

8.8
and
Eq.

8.9
into
Eq.

8.7
, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.016\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}n\,.

∎

Now combining
Lemma

8.5
and
Lemma

8.9
, we can prove
Theorem

8.10
.

Theorem 8.10
.

Consider the setting of
Algorithm

8.1
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{103.23824pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-103.23824pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[103.23824pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(1)}$}}}\hskip-103.23824pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[103.23824pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{\mathbf{1}-x^{(1)}\odot x^{(2)}}{2}}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{0.96-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\exp(-\frac{\gamma\tilde{C}}{2})+\delta_{\eta}}}\right)n}}\right)\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

8.5
, we can obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g)),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}(x^{(2)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g)),x^{(2)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\,.

Therefore, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}(x^{(2)}),x^{(2)}\odot w}}\right\rangle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}\odot g),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}(x^{(2)}\odot g),x^{(2)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\,.

By
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{maj}}(Y,Z,R;\gamma,\tilde{C})
, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}),x^{(1)}\odot w}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}(x^{(2)}),x^{(2)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\geqslant 2\alpha_{\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lVert w\rVert^{2}-\beta_{\gamma,\tilde{C}}n}}\right)\,.

By
Lemma

8.9
, we have for each
t\in\{1,2\}
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(t)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(t)}\odot g}}\right),x^{(t)}\odot w}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{0.016\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}n\,.

Therefore, we have

\alpha_{\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\lVert w\rVert^{2}-\beta_{\gamma,\tilde{C}}n}}\right)\leqslant\frac{0.016\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}n\,.

By rearranging terms and plugging in
\alpha_{\gamma}=\frac{(1-\gamma)\varepsilon d}{16k}
and
\beta_{\gamma,\tilde{C}}=\frac{640k}{1-\gamma}\exp(-\gamma\tilde{C}/2)
, we can obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{0.96-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\exp(-\frac{\gamma\tilde{C}}{2})+\delta_{\eta}}}\right)n}}\right)\,.

Since
w=v\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)
, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v}}\right\rVert^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w+v\odot s}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w}}\right\rVert^{2}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v\odot s}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{0.96-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\exp(-\frac{\gamma\tilde{C}}{2})+\delta_{\eta}}}\right)n}}\right)\,.

∎

8.4
Algorithmic guarantees for robust bisection boosting

Now, we are ready to prove
Theorem

5.4
.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

5.4
)
.

Let
(G^{\circ},Z^{\circ})\sim\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
be generated from the
k
-stochastic block model and
G
be generated by adversarially corrupting
\eta
-fraction of the nodes in
G^{\circ}
.
Assume
k\leqslant n^{0.001}
,
\eta\leqslant\frac{1}{\poly(k)}
,
d=o(n)
, and
\varepsilon^{2}d\geqslant Kk^{2}
for some sufficiently large constant
K
.
Let
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k/2}\subset V
be disjoint subsets of size
n/k
such that in every
S_{i}
at least
0.99
-fraction of the nodes belong to the same community.
Let
x^{\circ}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
be the ground-truth community bisection with the underlying communities of
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k/2}
on the same side.
There exists a polynomial-time algorithm that, given observation of
G
and
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k/2}
, outputs
\hat{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
such that, with probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(k))-\tfrac{1}{\poly(n)}
,

\frac{1}{n}\|\hat{x}-x^{\circ}\|^{2}\leqslant\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{\tilde{C}}{8}}}\right)+\poly(k)\eta\,,

where
\tilde{C}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{a^{\frac{2}{k}}b^{1-\frac{2}{k}}}-\sqrt{b}}}\right)^{2}
is the bisection SNR.

Proof.

Since the program is feasible for the spectrally truncated graph
\bar{G}^{\circ}\odot(\mathbf{1}_{\bar{S}}\mathbf{1}_{\bar{S}}^{\top})
with ground-truth label
Z^{\circ}
with the ground-truth bisection be
x^{\circ}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
, by
Theorem

8.10
, it follows that

\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\tilde{\E}x-x^{\circ}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k}{0.96-\gamma}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\gamma\tilde{C}}{4}\Bigr)+\delta_{\eta}}}\right)}}\right)\,,

where
\tilde{C}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{a^{\frac{2}{k}}b^{1-\frac{2}{k}}}-\sqrt{b}}}\right)^{2}
. Plug in
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
and
\delta_{\eta}=\exp(-2C_{d,\varepsilon})+\eta
, it follows that

\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\tilde{\E}x-x^{\circ}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\tilde{C}}{8}\Bigr)+\eta}}\right)}}\right)\,.

Let
\hat{x}=\sign(\tilde{\E}x)
. Since the entrywise error of sign rounding increases by at most a multiplicative factor of
O(1)
, it follows that

\frac{1}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\hat{x}-x^{\circ}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\tilde{C}}{8}\Bigr)+\eta}}\right)}}\right)=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{\tilde{C}}{8}}}\right)+O(k\eta)\,.

∎

9
Robust bisection algorithm

In this section, we combine results from
Section

6
,
Section

7
and
Section

8
to design an algorithm for robust bisectioning and prove
Theorem

5.5
.

Algorithm 9.1

(Robust bisection algorithm)
.

Input:
A graph
G
sampled from the
k
-stochastic block model
\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
with
\eta n
corrupted nodes.

1.

Graph splitting:
We let
G_{1}
be the graph obtained by subsampling each edge in
G
independently with probability
0.99
and let
G_{2}\coloneqq G\setminus G_{1}
.

2.

Rough initialization:
Apply the algorithm from
Theorem

5.2
on graph
G_{1}
to obtain a rough
k
-clustering
Z_{\text{rough}}
with error rate
0.001+10^{4}\eta
.

3.

Identifying well recovered blocks:
Apply the algorithm from
Theorem

5.3
on graph
G_{2}
and
Z_{\text{rough}}
to identify
k/2
clusters
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k/2}
in which
0.99
-fraction of the nodes belongs to the same community.

4.

Bisection boosting:
Apply the algorithm from
Theorem

5.4
on graph
G
with
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k/2}
and
Z_{\text{rough}}
as input, find a bisection
\hat{x}
that separates the identified blocks from the remaining blocks with an error rate
\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\,\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}}}\right)+\poly(k)\eta
.

Output:

\hat{x}
.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

5.5
)
.

Let
(G^{\circ},Z^{\circ})\sim\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
be generated from the
k
-stochastic block model and
G
be generated by adversarially corrupting
\eta
-fraction of the nodes in
G^{\circ}
.
Assume
k\leqslant n^{0.001}
,
\eta\leqslant\frac{1}{\poly(k)}
,
d=o(n)
, and
\varepsilon^{2}d\geqslant Kk^{2}
for some sufficiently large constant
K
.
There exists a polynomial-time algorithm that, given observation of
G
, outputs
\hat{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
such that, with probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(k))-\tfrac{1}{\poly(n)}
,

\frac{1}{n}\|\hat{x}-x^{\circ}\|^{2}\,\leqslant\,\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{\tilde{C}}{8}}}\right)+O(k\eta).

where
\tilde{C}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{a^{\frac{2}{k}}b^{1-\frac{2}{k}}}-\sqrt{b}}}\right)^{2}
is the bisection SNR and
x^{\circ}\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}
is a true community bisection of
G^{\circ}
.

Proof.

For
Theorem

5.4
, it suffices to establish the guarantees required by the initialization phase.
Specifically, we must obtain
k/2
disjoint subsets
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k/2}
, each of size exactly
n/k
,

15
15

15

We assume that
k
is a power of 2 and
n
is divisible by
k
,
ensuring that
n/k
is an integer and eliminating rounding issues in the partitioning process.
such that, with high probability,
at least a
0.99
fraction of the vertices within each subset belong to the same community.

By
Theorem

5.2
, we obtain with high probability disjoint subsets
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{k}\subseteq[n]
, such that for at least
0.99
fraction of these subsets,
each contains at least
0.999
fraction of its vertices from one ground-truth community,
with fewer than
0.001n/k
vertices from other communities.
Without loss of generality, let these well-aligned subsets be
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{0.99k}
.

To guarantee that each subset has exactly
n/k
vertices, we rebalance the subsets by redistributing vertices appropriately.
Specifically, surplus vertices are transferred from subsets exceeding size
n/k
to subsets with fewer than
n/k
vertices.
Let the resulting balanced subsets be denoted by
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k}
.

The key observation here is that, initially, each of the subsets
V_{1},\dots,V_{0.99k}
contains between
0.999n/k
and
1.001n/k
vertices.
Consequently, the number of vertices added to or removed from each of these subsets during rebalancing is at most
0.001n/k
.
Thus, each subset retains at least
0.998n/k
vertices from its dominant community.
Therefore, for at least
0.99
fraction of the subsets, a
0.998
fraction of their vertices still belongs to a single ground-truth community.

Now, we have
k/2
disjoint subsets
S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{k/2}
, each of size
n/k
, satisfying the condition that at least
0.998
fraction of vertices in each subset originate from the same community.
By
Theorem

5.3
, the algorithm will accept these subsets as valid clusters and reject subsets with recovery rates below
0.99
.
Combining this with
Theorem

5.4
concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

10
Robust initialization for symmetry breaking

Building on the robust optimal bisection algorithm from
Section

9
,
we present a robust polynomial-time algorithm for clustering vertices into
k
communities.
Our approach attains a misclassification error strictly below the critical
1/k
symmetry-breaking threshold.

Algorithm 10.1

(Robust
k
-clustering via recursive bisections)
.

Input:
Graph
G
drawn from the
k
-stochastic block model
\mathsf{SBM}_{n_{i}}(d_{i},\varepsilon,k_{i})
with corruption fraction at most
\eta_{i}
.

1.

Apply the robust bisection algorithm (
Algorithm

9.1
) on
G
to obtain bisection
x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n_{i}}
.

2.

Let
x^{+}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{i\in[n]:x_{i}=1\}
and
x^{-}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{i\in[n]:x_{i}=-1\}
. Partition
G
into two induced subgraphs
G_{1}=G[x^{+}]
and
G_{2}=G[x^{-}]
such that
each distrbutes as
\mathsf{SBM}_{n_{i+1}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d_{i+1},\varepsilon,k_{i+1}}}\right)
.

3.

Recursively apply Algorithm
10.1
to
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
with parameters
n_{i+1},d_{i+1},\varepsilon,k_{i+1}
.

4.

After
\lfloor\log k\rfloor
levels of recursion, merge the resulting clusters into a final community assignment vector
\hat{Z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times k}
.

Output:

\hat{Z}
.

Now, we restate and prove the algorithmic guarantees of
Algorithm

10.1
.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

5.6
)
.

Let
(G^{\circ},Z^{\circ})\sim\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
be generated from the
k
-stochastic block model and
G
be generated by adversarially corrupting
\eta
-fraction of the nodes in
G^{\circ}
.
Assume
k\leqslant n^{0.001}
,
\eta\leqslant\frac{1}{\poly(k)}
,
d=o(n)
, and
\varepsilon^{2}d\geqslant Kk^{2}
for some sufficiently large constant
K
.
There exists a polynomial-time algorithm that, given observation of
G
, outputs an estimator
\hat{Z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times k}
such that, with probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(k))-\tfrac{1}{\poly(n)}
,

\text{error}_{k}(\hat{Z},Z^{\circ})\,\leqslant\,\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\,\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}}}\right)+\poly(k)\eta.

Proof.

We follow the level-wise parametrization of Appendix
C.1
:
for level
i\in\{1,\dots,\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor\}
set

\beta_{i}\coloneqq 2^{-i}
,
n_{i}\coloneqq 2\beta_{i}n
, and
k_{i}\coloneqq\beta_{i}k
.
There are
2^{i}
subproblems at level
i
, each on
n_{i}
vertices and
k_{i}
communities.
Let
\widetilde{C}_{i}
be the level-
i
bisection SNR and
\mathrm{err}_{i}
the (per-vertex)
bisection error of
Algorithm

5.1
on a level-
i
subproblem.

The original instance has at most
\eta n
corrupted vertices.
Restricting to any level-
i
vertex set
V_{i}
of size
n_{i}
gives at most
\eta n
corruptions inside
V_{i}
, so the
effective corruption fraction satisfies

\eta_{i}\leqslant\frac{\eta n}{n_{i}}=\frac{\eta}{2\beta_{i}}\leqslant\frac{\eta}{\beta_{i}}\leqslant k\eta,

since
\beta_{i}\geqslant 1/k
for
i\leqslant\log_{2}k
.
Applied at level
i
,
Theorem

5.5
(with
k_{i}
communities and corruption
\eta_{i}
) gives, for some absolute
C_{0}>0
,

\mathrm{err}_{i}\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{\widetilde{C}_{i}}{8}\Bigr)+C_{0}k_{i}\eta_{i}.

By
Theorem

C.5
,
\beta_{i}\widetilde{C}_{i}
is nondecreasing in
i
and

\beta_{1}\widetilde{C}_{1}=\Theta(C_{d,\varepsilon}/k^{2})
, hence

\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{\widetilde{C}_{i}}{8}\Bigr)\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}\Bigr)\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover
k_{i}\eta_{i}\leqslant(\beta_{i}k)(\eta/\beta_{i})=k\eta
, so

\mathrm{err}_{i}\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}\Bigr)+C_{0}k\eta\quad\text{for all }i.

(10.1)

At level
i
there are
2^{i}
disjoint subproblems, each with error at most
\mathrm{err}_{i}
, so the number of newly
misclustered vertices at that level is at most
2^{i}\mathrm{err}_{i}n_{i}
.
Since
n_{i}=2\beta_{i}n=2^{1-i}n
, we have
2^{i}n_{i}=2n
, and the contribution of level
i
to the global error is at most

\frac{2^{i}\mathrm{err}_{i}n_{i}}{n}\leqslant 2\mathrm{err}_{i}.

Summing over
i=1,\dots,\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor
and using (
10.1
),

\text{error}_{k}(\hat{Z},Z^{\circ})\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor}2\mathrm{err}_{i}\leqslant O(\log k)\bigl(\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}\Bigr)+C_{0}k\eta\bigr).

Absorbing the
O(\log k)
and
C_{0}
factors into
\poly(k)
, we obtain

\text{error}_{k}(\hat{Z},Z^{\circ})\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k^{2}}\Bigr)+\poly(k)\,\eta,

as claimed.
∎

As a direct consequence of
Theorem

5.6
, we obtain the following corollary, capturing the symmetry-breaking improvement:

Corollary 10.2
.

If
d\varepsilon^{2}\geqslant Kk^{2}\log k
for some sufficiently large constant
K
, then
Algorithm

10.1
achieves, with probability at least
1-\exp(-\Omega(k))-\tfrac{1}{\poly(n)}
, a labeling error at most
O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\poly(k)}}}\right)
.

11
Robust optimal recovery algorithm

In this section, we build on the guarantees from
Section

10
and give a robust polynomial-time algorithm that partitions the vertices into
k
communities at the minimax-optimal rate. The algorithm is a constant-degree sum-of-squares (SoS) relaxation of polynomial constraints that encode pairwise majority voting. These constraints closely mirror those used for robust bisectioning. The key difference is the symmetry-breaking guarantee from
Corollary

10.2
, which ensures that across all feasible solutions the community labels are aligned. This alignment allows us to analyze the majority voting guarantees for each pair of communities.

11.1
Algorithm and constraint systems

We first introduce the polynomial constraints that are used in our robust optimal recovery algorithm.

(i) Labeling constraints.
The feasible label variable
Z\in\{0,1\}^{n\times k}
satisfies

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{label}}(Z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{Z(i,a)^{2}=Z(i,a),\quad\sum_{a}Z(i,a)=1,\quad\sum_{i}Z(i,a)=\frac{n}{k}}}\right\}\,.

(11.1)

(ii) Initialization (symmetry breaking).
Let
Z_{\mathrm{init}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be an initializer with
\mu
-approximation error. We constrain the SoS variable
Z
to remain close to
Z_{\mathrm{init}}
:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{sym\text{-}break}}(Z;Z_{\mathrm{init}},\mu)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z-Z_{\mathrm{init}}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leqslant\mu}}\right\}\,.

(11.2)

(iii) Corruption mask / node distance.
We model node corruptions through a binary mask
\xi\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and require the program matrix to agree with the observed graph outside the corrupted rows/columns:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{close}}(Y,\xi;\bar{G},\eta)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(Y-\bar{G})\odot(\mathbf{1}-\xi)(\mathbf{1}-\xi)^{\top}=0,\ \xi\odot\xi=\xi,\ \sum_{i}\xi_{i}\leqslant\delta_{\eta}n}}\right\}\,,

(11.3)

where
\delta_{\eta}=\exp(-2C_{d,\varepsilon})+\eta
.

(iv) Community structure and spectral condition.

We encode the decomposition of the centered adjacency into pairwise components and bound the spectral norm of the noise blocks:

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{mix}}(Y,Z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{X=ZZ^{\top}-\frac{1}{k}J,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Y-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}}}\right\}\,.

(11.4)

(v) Majority-vote consistency.

For each pair
a,b\in[k]
define
x_{ab}=Z(\cdot,a)-Z(\cdot,b)
.
We require that, the pairwise labels are satisfy majority voting constraint.

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{maj}}(Y,Z,R;\gamma,\beta)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{aligned} &R\colon\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{set}}(z)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.728pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.728pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.728pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.728pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.728pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{u}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Yx_{ab},z\odot x_{ab}}}\right\rangle\geqslant\frac{(1-\gamma)\varepsilon d}{16k}\Big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert z\odot x_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}-640\beta n\Big)\\
&x_{ab}=Z(\cdot,a)-Z(\cdot,b)\end{aligned}}}\right\}\,,

(11.5)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{set}}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\{z\odot z=z\}
.
We will use two parameter settings, corresponding to a two-round majority vote that achieves the minimax rate
16
16

16

Two rounds are needed to sharpen the error from a constant-factor exponent to the optimal exponent.

•

\mu_{1}=\frac{n}{\poly(k)}
,
\gamma_{1}=0.99
,
\beta_{1}=1000k\exp\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-0.99\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
.

•

\mu_{\text{opt}}=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-0.99(1-o(1))\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
,
\gamma_{\text{opt}}=1-\frac{10\chi k}{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}
,
\beta_{\text{opt}}=\frac{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}{10\chi}\exp\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{10\chi k}{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}}}\right)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
.

For our algorithm, we define polynomial system
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi,R;\bar{G},Z_{\mathrm{init}},\eta,\mu,\gamma,\beta)
based on the set of constraints defined above
17
17

17

For simplicity, we use
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
to denote
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi,R;\bar{G},Z_{\mathrm{init}},\eta,\gamma,\beta)
when the input is clear from the context.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi,R;\bar{G},Z_{\mathrm{init}},\eta,\mu,\gamma,\beta)\\
&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{label}}(Z)\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{sym\text{-}break}}(Z;Z_{\mathrm{init}},\mu)\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{close}}(Y,\xi;\bar{G},\eta)\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{mix}}(Y,Z)\cup\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{maj}}(Y,Z,R;\gamma,\beta)\,.\end{split}

(11.6)

Algorithm.

The complete robust optimal recovery algorithm for
k
-SBM is described in
Algorithm

11.1
.

Algorithm 11.1

(Robust recovery algorithm)
.

Input:
The centered adjacency matrix
\bar{G}
drawn from
\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
with at most
\eta n
corrupted nodes.

1.

Run
Algorithm

10.1
to obtain an initializer
\hat{Z}_{\text{init}}
.

2.

Find degree-
O(1)
pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\E}
satisfying
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi,R;\bar{G},Z_{\mathrm{init}},\eta,\gamma,\beta)
with
\hat{Z}_{\text{init}}
,
\gamma=0.99
,
\mu=\frac{n}{\poly(k)}
,
\beta=1000k\exp\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-0.99\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
.

3.

Round
\tilde{\E}
by labelling vertex
i
with label
\argmax_{a}\tilde{\E}[Z(i,a)]
to
\hat{Z}
.

4.

Find degree-
O(1)
pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\E}_{\text{opt}}
satisfying
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi,R;\bar{G},Z_{\mathrm{init}},\eta,\gamma,\beta)
with
\hat{Z}
,
\mu=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-0.99(1-o(1))\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
,
\gamma=1-\frac{10\chi k}{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}
,
\beta=\frac{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}{10\chi}\exp\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{10\chi k}{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}}}\right)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
.

5.

Round
\tilde{\E}_{\text{opt}}
by labelling vertex
i
with label
\argmax_{a}\tilde{\E}_{\text{opt}}[Z(i,a)]
to
Z_{\text{opt}}
.

Output:

Z_{\text{opt}}
.

11.2
Feasibility and time complexity

We first establish feasibility of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
and time complexity of
Algorithm

11.1
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
\bar{G}^{\circ}
be the centered adjacency matrix of the uncorrupted graph and
S
be the set of nodes with degree larger than
20d
.
Let
T
be the set of corrupted nodes.
Let
Z^{\circ}
be the ground truth label matrix.
Under the conditions of
Theorem

5.7
, program
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
is satisfied by
(\bar{G}^{\circ}\odot(\mathbf{1}_{\bar{S}}\mathbf{1}_{\bar{S}}^{\top}),Z^{\circ},\mathbf{1}_{S\cup T})
with probability at least
1-\exp(-100k)-1/n^{3}
.

Proof.

The proof parallels
Lemma

8.2
. For
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{mix}}
, feasibility follows from
Corollary

4.6
.
The mixing constraints
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Y-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}
follow from applying
Corollary

B.2
and similar analysis as
Lemma

8.2
.
The feasibility of initialization constraint follows from
Theorem

5.6
.
The feasibility of corruption constraint follows from
Corollary

B.2
.
∎

Lemma 11.3
.

Algorithm

11.1
runs in polynomial time.

Proof.

The SoS relaxation has constant degree and therefore produces an SDP with polynomially many variables and constraints, which can be solved in polynomial time.
∎

11.3
SoS guarantees for robust optimal boosting

We now prove
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
boosts the accuracy to the desired rate.
To do this, we consider two feasible solutions
(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)})
and
(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)})
of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
.
We will show that the constraints in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
allow us to prove that any two good solutions will be within minimax error rate to each other.
Since
Z^{\circ}
is also a good solution, this implies that any feasible solution of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
achieves the minimax error rate.

Throughout this section, we define the following terms:

•

Let
l_{ab}^{(t)}=x_{ab}^{(t)}\odot x_{ab}^{(t)}
be the support of
x_{ab}^{(t)}
for
t\in\{1,2\}
. Notice that, equivalently,
l_{ab}^{(t)}=Z^{(t)}(\cdot,a)+Z^{(t)}(\cdot,b)
.

•

Let
l_{ab}=l_{ab}^{(1)}\odot l_{ab}^{(2)}
be the intersection of
l_{ab}^{(1)}
and
l_{ab}^{(2)}
.

•

Let
v_{ab}=\frac{l_{ab}^{(1)}\odot l_{ab}^{(2)}-x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot x_{ab}^{(2)}}{2}
be the coordinates where two feasible pairwise labels
x_{ab}^{(1)}
and
x_{ab}^{(2)}
differ.

•

Let
s=\mathbf{1}-(\mathbf{1}-\xi^{(1)})\odot(\mathbf{1}-\xi^{(2)})
be the set of nodes where
Y^{(1)}
and
Y^{(2)}
differ.

•

Let
w_{ab}=v_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)
be the set of vertices in the shared part of
Y^{(1)}
and
Y^{(2)}
such that the bisections
x^{(1)}
and
x^{(2)}
differ.

•

Let
g_{ab}=\mathbf{1}-(\mathbf{1}-v_{ab})\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)
be union of
v_{ab}
and
s
, notice that, equivalently
g_{ab}=w_{ab}+s
.

To prove the algorithmic guarantee of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}(Y,Z,\xi)
, we need the following observations on properties of the variables defined above.

Lemma 11.4
.

For any
a,b\in[k]
,
x^{(1)}_{ab}
,
x^{(2)}_{ab}
and
v_{ab}
satisfies

x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}=-x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}\quad\text{and}\quad x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot(l_{ab}-v_{ab})=x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot(l_{ab}-v_{ab})\,,

and,

v_{ab}\odot\frac{l_{ab}-x^{(1)}_{ab}}{2}=v_{ab}\odot\frac{l_{ab}+x^{(2)}_{ab}}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad v_{ab}\odot\frac{l_{ab}-x^{(2)}_{ab}}{2}=v_{ab}\odot\frac{l_{ab}+x^{(1)}_{ab}}{2}\,.

Proof.

The proof follows by similar analysis as
Lemma

8.4
.
∎

Lemma 11.5
.

The norm of
v_{ab}
,
s
and
g_{ab}
satisfies the following SoS inequalities

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{32.18074pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-32.18074pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[32.18074pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(1)}$}}}\hskip-32.18074pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[32.18074pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert s}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2\delta_{\eta}n\,,

and,

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{36.78072pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-36.78072pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[36.78072pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(1)}$}}}\hskip-36.78072pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[36.78072pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Z^{(1)},Z^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mu\,,

and,

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{67.22542pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-67.22542pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[67.22542pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(1)}$}}}\hskip-67.22542pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[67.22542pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Z^{(1)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mu\,,

and,

\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{67.22542pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-67.22542pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[67.22542pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(1)}$}}}\hskip-67.22542pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[67.22542pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Z^{(1)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert g_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mu+4\delta_{\eta}n\,.

Proof.

For the bound on
s
, by constraints in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{close}}(Y,\xi;\eta,\bar{G})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert s}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}s_{i}=\sum_{i}1-(1-\xi^{(1)}_{i})\odot(1-\xi^{(2)}_{i})=\sum_{i}\xi^{(1)}_{i}+\sum_{i}\xi^{(2)}_{i}(1-\xi^{(1)}_{i})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i}\xi^{(1)}_{i}+\sum_{i}\xi^{(2)}_{i}\leqslant 2\delta_{\eta}n\,.

For the bound on
v_{ab}
, by constraints in
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{init}}(Z;Z_{\mathrm{init}})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}=

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{l_{ab}^{(1)}\odot l_{ab}^{(2)}-x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot x_{ab}^{(2)}}{2}}}\right\rVert^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot l_{ab}^{(2)}-x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot l_{ab}^{(1)}}{2}}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\frac{x_{ab}^{(1)}-x_{ab}^{(2)}}{2}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z^{(1)}(\cdot,a)-Z^{(2)}(\cdot,a)}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z^{(1)}(\cdot,b)-Z^{(2)}(\cdot,b)}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z^{(1)}(\cdot,a)-Z_{\mathrm{init}}(\cdot,a)}}\right\rVert^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z^{(2)}(\cdot,a)-Z_{\mathrm{init}}(\cdot,a)}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z^{(1)}(\cdot,b)-Z_{\mathrm{init}}(\cdot,b)}}\right\rVert^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z^{(2)}(\cdot,b)-Z_{\mathrm{init}}(\cdot,b)}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert Z-Z_{\mathrm{init}}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leqslant\mu\,.

The bound on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}
follows by

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mu\,.

For the bound on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert g_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}
, since
g_{ab}=w_{ab}+s
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert g_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}+s}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert s}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mu+4\delta_{\eta}n\,.

∎

Fact 11.6
.

Fix
a,b\in[k]
, there is a constant-degree SoS proof of

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\,(x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\;=\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}\,(x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\,.

Proof.

Plugging in the definition of
g_{ab}
and
w_{ab}
, the left hand side can be written as

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-v_{ab})\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)),x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-v_{ab})}}\right),x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}}}\right\rangle\,.

By
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{close}}(Y,\xi;\eta,\bar{G})
, we have

Y^{(1)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}=\bar{G}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}=Y^{(2)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}\,,

and, by
Lemma

11.4
, we have

x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}=-x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}\quad\text{and}\quad x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot(l_{ab}-v_{ab})=x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot(l_{ab}-v_{ab})\,,

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}(x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x^{(1)}_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-s)(\mathbf{1}-s)^{\top}}}\right)(x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-v_{ab})),x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot v_{ab}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}(x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x^{(2)}_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\,.

∎

We next upper bound the contribution of the signal on the set
g
.

Lemma 11.7
.

In the setting of
Theorem

5.7
, for any
\delta\in[0,0.001]
and
t\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{103.23824pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-103.23824pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[103.23824pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{6}$}}}\hskip-103.23824pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[103.23824pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(t)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(t)}_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x^{(t)}_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon d}{\poly(k)}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}{k}n\,.

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we ignore the superscript
(t)
throughout the proof. We decompose the left hand side in the following way

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle=\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle X\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle(Y-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\,.

(11.7)

For the first term, since
X=ZZ^{\top}-\frac{1}{k}J
, we have
-J\leqslant X\leqslant J
such that

\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle X\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}(g_{ab})_{i}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}(w_{ab})_{i}}}\right)=\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert g_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

Since
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert g_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\mu+4\delta_{\eta}n
, it follows that

\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle X\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon d\mu}{n}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\,.

(11.8)

For the second term, by
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{mix}}(Y,Z)
and SoS Cauchy-Schwarz, we also have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle(Y-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert s}}\right\rVert^{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\chi+\frac{1}{k}}}\right)\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(3\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+2\delta_{\eta}n}}\right)\,.

Since
\sqrt{d}\ll\frac{\varepsilon d}{k}
, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon d}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}{k}n\,.

(11.9)

Plugging
Eq.

11.8
and
Eq.

11.9
into
Eq.

11.7
, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{ab}\odot g}}\right),x_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon d\mu}{n}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}{k}n\,.

∎

Now, we are ready to prove the algorithmic guarantee of
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}
.

Theorem 11.8
.

In the setting of
Theorem

5.7
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)}}}\right),\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{opt}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}}\right)

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{103.23824pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-103.23824pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[103.23824pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{6}$}}}\hskip-103.23824pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[103.23824pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{Y^{(1)},Z^{(1)},\xi^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(2)},\xi^{(2)}}$}}}$}}}}\sum_{a,b}\lVert v_{ab}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)k^{2}\beta n+k^{2}\eta n}{1-\gamma-\mu k/n-\eta k}+k^{2}\mu n}}\right)\,.

Proof.

For any pairs of communities
a,b\in[k]
, by
11.6
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\,(x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\;=\;-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}\,(x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot(\mathbf{1}-g_{ab})),x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\,.

Therefore,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}x_{ab}^{(1)},x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}x_{ab}^{(2)},x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(1)}\!\big(x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot g\big),x_{ab}^{(1)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(2)}\!\big(x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot g\big),x_{ab}^{(2)}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle.

(11.10)

By
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{maj}}(Y,Z,R;\gamma,\beta)
, it follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(t)}x^{(t)}_{ab},x^{(t)}_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\geqslant\frac{(1-\gamma)\varepsilon d}{16k}\Big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}-640\beta n\Big)\,,

(11.11)

for
t\in\{1,2\}
.
By
Lemma

11.7
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle Y^{(t)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(t)}_{ab}\odot g_{ab}}}\right),x^{(t)}_{ab}\odot w_{ab}}}\right\rangle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon d\mu}{n}+4\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon d\delta_{\eta}}{k}n\,.

(11.12)

for
t\in\{1,2\}
.

Therefore, plugging
Eq.

11.11
and
Eq.

11.12
into
Eq.

11.10
and rearranging terms, we can obtain

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant\frac{(1-\gamma)\beta n+\eta n}{1-\gamma-\mu k/n-\eta k}\,.

Since
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert v_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}\leqslant 2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert w_{ab}}}\right\rVert^{2}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert s}}\right\rVert^{2}
, it follows that

\sum_{a,b}\lVert v_{ab}\rVert^{2}\leqslant O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(1-\gamma)k^{2}\beta n+k^{2}\eta n}{1-\gamma-\mu k/n-\eta k}+k^{2}\mu n}}\right)\,.

∎

11.4
Algorithmic guarantees for robust optimal recovery

In this section, we prove our main theorem and establish the algorithmic guarantees of our robust optimal recovery algorithm for
k
-SBM.

Theorem

(Restatement of
Theorem

5.7
)
.

Let
(G^{\circ},Z^{\circ})\sim\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
be generated from the
k
-stochastic block model and
G
be generated by adversarially corrupting
\eta
-fraction of the nodes in
G^{\circ}
.
Assume
k\leqslant n^{0.001}
,
\eta\leqslant\frac{1}{\poly(k)}
,
d=o(n)
, and
\varepsilon^{2}d\geqslant Kk^{2}\log k
for some sufficiently large constant
K
.
There exists a polynomial-time algorithm (see
Algorithm

11.1
) that, given observation of
G
, outputs an estimator
\hat{Z}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times k}
such that, with probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(k))-\tfrac{1}{\poly(n)}
,

\text{error}_{k}(\hat{Z},Z^{\circ})\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(1-o(1)\bigr)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}\Bigr)+\poly(k)\cdot\eta\,.

Proof.

Let
x^{\circ}_{ab}
be the the ground-truth pairwise labels.
Notice that
Algorithm

10.1
provides a polynomial-time computable initializer
\hat{Z}_{\text{input}}
with error at most
n/\poly(k)
that we can use in step 2 of
Algorithm

11.1
.
Plugging in
Z_{\text{input}}=\hat{Z}_{\text{input}}
,
\mu=\frac{n}{\poly(k)}
,
\gamma=0.99
and
\beta=1000k\exp\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-0.99\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
in
Theorem

11.8
and rearranging terms, we can obtain
\tilde{\E}
such that

\sum_{a,b}\lVert\tilde{\E}x^{\circ}_{ab}-x_{ab}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-(1-o(1))\frac{0.99C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)n+\poly(k)\eta n\,.

Since the rounding introduces a constant multiplicative error, we can use the rounded solution
\hat{Z}
in step 3.
Repeating
Theorem

11.8
with
Z_{\text{input}}=\hat{Z}
,
\mu=\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-0.99(1-o(1))\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
,
\gamma=1-\frac{10\chi k}{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}
,
\beta=\frac{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}{10\chi}\exp\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{10\chi k}{\sqrt{C_{d,\varepsilon}}}}}\right)\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)
, we can obtain
\tilde{\E}_{\text{opt}}
such that

\sum_{a,b}\lVert\tilde{\E}_{\text{opt}}x^{\circ}_{ab}-x_{ab}\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-(1-o(1))\frac{C_{d,\varepsilon}}{k}}}\right)n+\poly(k)\eta n\,.

Combine this with the rounding guarantees, we obtain the desired bound.
∎

Appendix A
Sum-of-Squares proofs

In this section, we introduce some Sum-of-Squares (SoS) results that are used in our proofs.

A.1
Basic Sum-of-Squares inequalities

We start with a Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for pseudo-distributions.

Fact A.1

(Cauchy-Schwarz for pseudo-distributions
[
9
]
)
.

Let
f,g
be vector polynomials of degree at most
d
in indeterminate
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, for any level
2d
pseudo-distribution
D
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\E}_{D}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\langle f,g\rangle}}\right]\leqslant\sqrt{\tilde{\E}_{D}[\lVert f\rVert^{2}]}\cdot\sqrt{\tilde{\E}_{D}[\lVert g\rVert^{2}]}\,.

We will also repeatedly use the following two SoS versions of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

Fact A.2

(Sum-of-Squares Cauchy-Schwarz I)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be indeterminates. Then,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle x,y}}\right\rangle^{2}\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{2}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}y_{i}^{2}}}\right)\,.

Fact A.3

(Sum-of-Squares Cauchy-Schwarz II)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be indeterminates. Then, for any
C>0
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle x,y}}\right\rangle\leqslant\frac{C}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert x}}\right\rVert^{2}+\frac{1}{2C}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert y}}\right\rVert^{2}\,,

and,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle x,y}}\right\rangle\geqslant-\frac{C}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert x}}\right\rVert^{2}-\frac{1}{2C}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert y}}\right\rVert^{2}\,,

We will use the following fact that shows how spectral certificates are captured within the SoS proof system.

Fact A.4

(Spectral Certificates)
.

For any
m\times m
matrix
A
,

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.728pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.728pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.728pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.728pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.728pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{u}$}}}$}}}\langle u,Au\rangle\leqslant\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert A}}\right\rVert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert u}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\,.

Combining these facts, we have the following lemma as a corollary:

Lemma A.5
.

For any constant
L\geqslant 0
, for variables
E\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},v\in\mathbb{R}^{n},w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have constant degree SoS proof that

L\mathrm{Id}-EE^{\top}=CC^{\top}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{33.06723pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-33.06723pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[33.06723pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-33.06723pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[33.06723pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{E,C,v,w}$}}}$}}}\langle Ev,w\rangle^{2}\leqslant L\lVert v\rVert^{2}\lVert w\rVert^{2}\,.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{33.06723pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-33.06723pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[33.06723pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-33.06723pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[33.06723pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{E,C,v,w}$}}}$}}}\

\displaystyle\langle Ev,w\rangle^{2}\leqslant\lVert v\rVert^{2}\lVert E^{\top}w\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle w,EE^{\top}w\rangle\cdot\lVert v\rVert^{2}\,.

Since
L\mathrm{Id}-EE^{\top}=CC^{\top}
, we have
\langle w,EE^{\top}w\rangle\leqslant L\lVert w\rVert^{2}
. As a result,

L\mathrm{Id}-EE^{\top}=CC^{\top}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{33.06723pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-33.06723pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[33.06723pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-33.06723pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[33.06723pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{E,C,v,w}$}}}$}}}\langle Ev,w\rangle^{2}\leqslant L\lVert v\rVert^{2}\lVert w\rVert^{2}\,.

∎

A.2
Sum-of-Squares certificate

Theorem A.6
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x),\ldots,p_{m}(x)
be a set of polynomials
\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
If there exists
y^{\circ}
such that
p_{1}(x)\geqslant 0,p_{2}(x)\geqslant 0,\ldots,p_{m}(x)\geqslant 0\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}q(x;y^{\circ})\geqslant 0
,
then the degree-
(\ell+2)
SoS relaxation of the following program is feasible

\mathcal{A}:=\Big\{p_{1}(x)\geqslant 0,p_{2}(x)\geqslant 0,\ldots,p_{m}(x)\geqslant 0,q(x,y)=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(RR^{\top}\mathrm{Mom}_{d}(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{m})}}\right)\Big\}\,,

where
\mathrm{Mom}_{d}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
is a block-diagonal matrix with entries in
\mathbb{R}[x]
and (possibly empty) blocks
M_{S}
indexed by subsets
S\subseteq[m]
such that
for all pairs of monomials
x^{\alpha},x^{\beta}
with
\deg x^{\alpha},\deg x^{\beta}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot(\ell-\sum_{i\in S}\deg p_{i})
,

M_{S}(\alpha,\beta)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod\nolimits_{i\in S}p_{i}}}\right)\cdot x^{\alpha}\cdot x^{\beta}\,.

We also have the following SoS proof

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{23.4293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-23.4293pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[23.4293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell+2}$}}}\hskip-23.4293pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[23.4293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y,R}$}}}$}}}q(x,y)\geqslant 0\,.

Proof.

Let
R^{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\ell}\times d^{\ell}}
be the matrix corresponding to a degree-
\ell
SoS proof of
p_{1}(x)\geqslant 0,p_{2}(x)\geqslant 0,\ldots,p_{m}(x)\geqslant 0\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}q(x;y^{\circ})\geqslant 0
, i.e.

q(x;y^{\circ})=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R^{\circ}(R^{\circ})^{\top}\mathrm{Mom}_{d}(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{m})}}\right)\,,

Now, the degree-
(\ell+2)
SoS relaxation of
\mathcal{A}
is feasible with
R=R^{\circ}
and
y=y^{\circ}
. The SoS proof follows by

\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{23.4293pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-23.4293pt\raisebox{-6.82222pt}{\makebox[23.4293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ell+2}$}}}\hskip-23.4293pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[23.4293pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y,R}$}}}$}}}q(x,y)=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(RR^{\top}\mathrm{Mom}_{d}(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{m})}}\right)\geqslant 0\,.

∎

A.3
Sum-of-Squares subset sum

Next we give an SoS proof for a formulation related to the minimum subset sum.

Lemma A.7
.

For any vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that for any subset
S\subseteq[n]
we have
\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}\geqslant\gamma(|S|-\beta)
, there is a SoS proof that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{z\odot z=z}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\langle z,v\rangle\geqslant\gamma(\lVert z\rVert_{1}-\beta)\,.

Proof.

We note that lemma is equivalent of showing

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{z\odot z=z}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\langle z,v-\gamma\mathbf{1}\rangle\geqslant-\gamma\cdot\beta\,.

If
v_{i}\leqslant\gamma
for all
i
, then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{z\odot z=z}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\langle z,v-\gamma\mathbf{1}\rangle\geqslant\langle\mathbf{1},v-\gamma\mathbf{1}\rangle\geqslant-\gamma\cdot\beta\,.

Else, w.l.o.g assume that
v
is in non-decreasing order and
v_{k}
is the last element that is at most
\gamma
, i.e.
v_{1}\leqslant v_{2}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant v_{k}\leqslant\gamma<v_{k+1}\leqslant v_{n}
. Now, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{z\odot z=z}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\langle z,v-\gamma\mathbf{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(v_{i}-\gamma)z_{i}+\sum_{i=k+1}^{n}(v_{i}-\gamma)z_{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{k}(v_{i}-\gamma)

\displaystyle\geqslant-\gamma\cdot\beta\,.

where the second last inequality is because
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{z\odot z=z}}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.10762pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.10762pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.10762pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{z}$}}}$}}}\mathbf{0}\leqslant z\leqslant\mathbf{1}
.
∎

Appendix B
Spectral bounds

A well-known challenge in analyzing sparse SBMs is that high-degree nodes can inflate spectral bounds by an additional
\log n
factor.
To establish identifiability in
Section

8
, we leverage a classical result from random matrix theory, which demonstrates that pruning nodes with degrees above a certain threshold suffices to achieve the desired spectral bounds.

Theorem B.1

(Originally proved in
[
25
]
, restatement of theorem 6.7 in
[
38
]
)
.

Suppose
M
is a random symmetric matrix with zero on the diagonal whose entries above the diagonal are independent with the following distribution

M_{ij}=\begin{cases}1-p_{ij}&\text{ w.p. }p_{ij}\\
-p_{ij}&\text{ w.p. }1-p_{ij}\end{cases}

Let
\sigma
be a quantity such that
p_{ij}\leqslant\sigma^{2}
and
M_{1}
be the matrix obtained from M by zeroing out
all the rows and columns having more than
20\sigma^{2}n
positive entries. Then with probability
1-1/n^{2}
, we have
\lVert M_{1}\rVert\leqslant\chi\sigma\sqrt{n}
for some universal constant
\chi
.

As a consequence of the above, we admit the following spectral bound for the adjacency matrix of a pruned SBM.

Corollary B.2

(Corollary 6.8 in
[
38
]
)
.

Let
G^{\circ}
be a graph sampled from the
k
-stochastic block model
\mathsf{SBM}_{n}(d,\varepsilon,k)
and
Z^{\circ}
be the true community membership matrix. Then, with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{n^{2}}
, there exists a subset
S\subseteq[n]
of size at least
\bigl(1-\exp{\bigl({-2C_{d,\varepsilon}}\bigr)}\bigr)n
such that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G^{\circ}-\frac{d}{n}J-\frac{\varepsilon d}{n}X^{\circ}}}\right)\odot\mathbf{1}_{S}\mathbf{1}_{S}^{\top}}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\chi\sqrt{d},

where
\chi
is a universal constant and
X^{\circ}=Z^{\circ}(Z^{\circ})^{\top}-\frac{1}{k}J
is the signal part of
G^{\circ}
.

Appendix C
Statistical properties of stochastic block model

C.1
Level-wise SBM statistics.

Let us consider a level
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor\}
in the recursive bisection as described in
Section

10
.
We define the parameters

\beta_{i}\coloneqq 2^{-i},\qquad n_{i}\coloneqq 2\beta_{i}n,\qquad k_{i}\coloneqq\beta_{i}k,

so that one side of the level-
i
bisection has
n_{i}/2
vertices and contains
k_{i}
communities.
We let
\alpha_{i}\coloneqq 1/k
denote the mass of a single ground-truth community and define the mixing parameter

\gamma_{i}\coloneqq\alpha_{i}/\beta_{i}\in[0,1],

as the fraction of the side occupied by the community of a fixed vertex.
Using
\gamma_{i}
, we introduce the
geometrically weighted
connection probabilities at level
i
, so that

\widetilde{a}_{i}\coloneqq a^{\gamma_{i}}b^{\,1-\gamma_{i}}\kern 5.0pt,\qquad\widetilde{b}_{i}\coloneqq b\kern 5.0pt.

This geometric weighting reflects that a random neighbor on the same side belongs to the relevant community with mass
\gamma_{i}

and to an irrelevant community with mass
1-\gamma_{i}
.
A simple uniform bound on the mixed probabilities will be used repeatedly through the section.

Lemma C.1
.

Fix
k\geqslant 2
and
0<\varepsilon<1
. Then, for every level
i
,

\sqrt{\widetilde{a}_{i}+\widetilde{b}_{i}}\leqslant\sqrt{a+b}=\sqrt{\,d\bigl(2+\varepsilon(1-\frac{2}{k})\bigr)\,}\leqslant\sqrt{3d}.

(C.1)

Proof.

Since
a\geqslant b
and
\gamma_{i}\in[0,1]
, we have
b^{\,1-\gamma_{i}}\leqslant a^{\,1-\gamma_{i}}
and hence

\widetilde{a}_{i}=a^{\gamma_{i}}b^{\,1-\gamma_{i}}\leqslant a^{\gamma_{i}}a^{\,1-\gamma_{i}}=a
; also
\widetilde{b}_{i}=b
.
Therefore
\widetilde{a}_{i}+\widetilde{b}_{i}\leqslant a+b
, which implies the first inequality in (
C.1
).
A direct computation gives

a+b=\Bigl(1+\bigl(1-\frac{1}{k}\bigr)\varepsilon+1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Bigr)d=\bigl(2+\varepsilon(1-\frac{2}{k})\bigr)d\leqslant(2+\varepsilon)d\leqslant 3d.

∎

To encode the same-side vs. opposite-side separation in a form amenable to multiplicative bounds used throughout the section
we consider the per-edge log-odds ratio. For
p,q\in(0,1)
set

R(p,q)\coloneqq\frac{p(1-q)}{q(1-p)}\,,\qquad\widetilde{R}_{i}\coloneqq R\Bigl(\widetilde{a_{i}}/n,\,\widetilde{b_{i}}/n\Bigr).

The following lemma provides a useful lower bound on
\log\widetilde{R}_{i}
.

Lemma C.2
.

Fix
k\geqslant 2
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and let
d=o(n)
.
Then, for every level
i
,

\frac{\gamma_{i}\,\varepsilon}{2}\leqslant\log\widetilde{R}_{i}\leqslant\gamma_{i}\,\varepsilon\log 3+o(1),

(C.2)

where
o(1)=o_{n}(1)
is uniform in
i
.

Proof.

By definition,

\log\widetilde{R}_{i}=\log\frac{\widetilde{a}_{i}}{\widetilde{b}_{i}}+\log\frac{1-\widetilde{b}_{i}/n}{1-\widetilde{a}_{i}/n}.

Lower bound.

Since
\widetilde{a}_{i}/\widetilde{b}_{i}=(a/b)^{\gamma_{i}}
and
\widetilde{a}_{i}\geqslant\widetilde{b}_{i}
,

\log\widetilde{R}_{i}\geqslant\gamma_{i}\log\frac{a}{b}.

With
a=(1+(1-\frac{1}{k})\varepsilon)d
,
b=(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k})d
,

\log\frac{a}{b}=\log\bigl(1+(1-\tfrac{1}{k})\varepsilon\bigr)-\log\bigl(1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{k}\bigr)\geqslant(1-\tfrac{1}{k})\varepsilon-\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\tfrac{1}{k})^{2}\varepsilon^{2}+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{k}\geqslant\varepsilon-\tfrac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\geqslant\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2},

hence
\log\widetilde{R}_{i}\geqslant\gamma_{i}\varepsilon/2
.

Upper bound.

Using
1-\widetilde{b}_{i}/n\leqslant 1
and
-\log(1-x)\leqslant x/(1-x)
for
x\in(0,1)
,

\log\frac{1-\widetilde{b}_{i}/n}{1-\widetilde{a}_{i}/n}\leqslant-\log\Bigl(1-\frac{\widetilde{a}_{i}}{n}\Bigr)\leqslant\frac{\widetilde{a}_{i}}{n-\widetilde{a}_{i}}\leqslant\frac{a}{n-a},

so

\log\widetilde{R}_{i}\leqslant\gamma_{i}\log\frac{a}{b}+\frac{a}{n-a}.

Moreover, for
k\geqslant 2
,

\log\frac{a}{b}=\log\frac{1+(1-\tfrac{1}{k})\varepsilon}{1-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{k}}\leqslant\log\frac{1+\varepsilon/2}{1-\varepsilon/2}\leqslant\varepsilon\log 3\kern 5.0pt.

We, thus, get

\log\widetilde{R}_{i}\leqslant\gamma_{i}\,\varepsilon\log 3+\frac{a}{n-a}.

Since
a=\Theta(d)
and
d=o(n)
, we have
a/(n-a)=o(1)
uniformly in
i
, yielding the desired upper bound.
∎

C.2
Concentration inequalities

We now prove
Theorem

C.5
,
a bound on the success probability of majority voting for the held-out vertex.
Let us start with a short convexity lemma.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
a,b,n>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
Assume
a\leqslant n
and
b\leqslant n
.
Define
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
by

f(x)=x-\bigl(x+\frac{n}{a}-1\bigr)^{\gamma}\bigl(x+\frac{n}{b}-1\bigr)^{1-\gamma}+\bigl(\frac{n}{a}\bigr)^{\gamma}\bigl(\frac{n}{b}\bigr)^{1-\gamma}-1.

Then
f(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x\in[0,1]
.

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

If
\gamma=1
then
f(x)\equiv 0
, thus we restrict attention to
0<\gamma<1
.
We certify nonnegativity by showing that
f
is convex on
[0,1]
and minimized at
x=1
.

Let
u(x)=x+\frac{n}{a}-1
and
v(x)=x+\frac{n}{b}-1
.
A direct differentiation gives

f^{\prime}(x)=1-\gamma\,u(x)^{\gamma-1}\,v(x)^{\,1-\gamma}-(1-\gamma)\,u(x)^{\gamma}\,v(x)^{-\gamma}.

(C.3)

Evaluating (
C.3
) at
x=1
(so
u(1)=\frac{n}{a}
,
v(1)=\frac{n}{b}
) yields

f^{\prime}(1)=1-\gamma\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{a}{b}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}-(1-\gamma)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{b}{a}}}\right)^{\gamma}\leqslant 0,

where we used the weighted AM-GM inequality

\gamma a+(1-\gamma)\,b\geqslant a^{\gamma}\,b^{\,1-\gamma}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\gamma\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{a}{b}}}\right)^{1-\gamma}+(1-\gamma)\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{b}{a}}}\right)^{\gamma}\geqslant 1.

Differentiating (
C.3
) once more and factoring,

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\gamma(1-\gamma)\,u(x)^{\gamma-2}\,v(x)^{\,1-\gamma}-2\,\gamma(1-\gamma)\,u(x)^{\gamma-1}\,v(x)^{-\gamma}+\gamma(1-\gamma)\,u(x)^{\gamma}\,v(x)^{-\gamma-1}

\displaystyle=\gamma(1-\gamma)\,u(x)^{\gamma-2}\,v(x)^{-\gamma-1}\,\bigl(v(x)-u(x)\bigr)^{2}\geqslant 0.

Hence
f
is convex on
[0,1]
.
In particular,
f^{\prime}
is nondecreasing on the interval.
Since
f^{\prime}(1)\leqslant 0
, we have
f^{\prime}(x)\leqslant 0
for all
x\in[0,1]
, i.e.,
f
is decreasing on
[0,1]
.
Finally,

f(1)=1-\Bigl(\frac{n}{a}\Bigr)^{\gamma}\Bigl(\frac{n}{b}\Bigr)^{1-\gamma}+\Bigl(\frac{n}{a}\Bigr)^{\gamma}\Bigl(\frac{n}{b}\Bigr)^{1-\gamma}-1=0,

so
f(x)\geqslant f(1)=0
for every
x\in[0,1]
, as claimed.
∎

Theorem C.4
.

Assume
a\leqslant n
and
b\leqslant n
. Fix parameters
\beta\in(0,1]
and
\alpha\in(0,\beta]
.
Consider the distribution

\mathcal{D}=\mathrm{Binom}(\alpha n,a/n)+\mathrm{Binom}\bigl((\beta-\alpha)n,\,b/n\bigr)-\mathrm{Binom}(\beta n,\,b/n)\kern 5.0pt,

where the three binomials are independent.
Let
\gamma\coloneqq\alpha/\beta
and define

\widetilde{a}\coloneqq a^{\gamma}b^{\,1-\gamma}\kern 5.0pt,\qquad\widetilde{b}\coloneqq b\kern 5.0pt,\qquad\widetilde{C}\coloneqq\bigl(\sqrt{\widetilde{a}}-\sqrt{\widetilde{b}}\,\bigr)^{2}\kern 5.0pt,\qquad R(p,q)\coloneqq\frac{p(1-q)}{q(1-p)}\kern 5.0pt.

Then for every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\ProbOp_{\bm{X}\sim\mathcal{D}}[\,\bm{X}\leqslant\theta\,]\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\,\beta\,\widetilde{C}+\frac{\theta}{2}\,\log R(\widetilde{a}/n,\,\widetilde{b}/n)\Bigr)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of Theorem
C.4
.

Let
t>0
to be fixed later.
By Markov’s inequality,

\ProbOp[\bm{X}\leqslant\theta]=\ProbOp\bigl(e^{-t\bm{X}}\geqslant e^{-t\theta}\bigr)\leqslant\E[e^{-t\bm{X}}]\,e^{t\theta}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the three binomials are independent and

\E[e^{s\,\mathrm{Binom}(m,p)}]=(pe^{s}+1-p)^{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\E[e^{-t\bm{X}}]

\displaystyle=\bigl(a/n\,e^{-t}+1-a/n\bigr)^{\alpha n}\bigl(b/n\,e^{-t}+1-b/n\bigr)^{(\beta-\alpha)n}\cdot\bigl(b/n\,e^{\,t}+1-b/n\bigr)^{\beta n}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\underbrace{\bigl(a/n\,e^{-t}+1-a/n\bigr)^{\gamma}\bigl(b/n\,e^{-t}+1-b/n\bigr)^{1-\gamma}}_{\text{apply Lemma \ref{lemma:concav_ineq}}}\cdot\bigl(b/n\,e^{\,t}+1-b/n\bigr)\Bigr)^{\beta n}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\gamma=\alpha/\beta\in[0,1]
.
Applying Lemma
C.3
with
x=e^{-t}\in(0,1]
gives

\bigl(a/n\,e^{-t}+1-a/n\bigr)^{\gamma}\bigl(b/n\,e^{-t}+1-b/n\bigr)^{1-\gamma}\leqslant(\widetilde{a}/n)\,e^{-t}+1-\widetilde{a}/n,

with
\widetilde{a}=a^{\gamma}b^{\,1-\gamma}
and
\widetilde{b}=b
. Hence

\E[e^{-t\bm{X}}]\leqslant\Bigl(\bigl(\frac{\widetilde{a}}{n}e^{-t}+1-\frac{\widetilde{a}}{n}\bigr)\bigl(\frac{\widetilde{b}}{n}e^{\,t}+1-\frac{\widetilde{b}}{n}\bigr)\Bigr)^{\beta n}\kern 5.0pt.

We choose

e^{t}=\sqrt{\frac{(\widetilde{a}/n)(1-\widetilde{b}/n)}{(\widetilde{b}/n)(1-\widetilde{a}/n)}}=\sqrt{R(\widetilde{a}/n,\,\widetilde{b}/n)}\kern 5.0pt.

It then follows that

\displaystyle\ProbOp[\bm{X}\leqslant\theta]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigl(\bigl(\frac{\widetilde{a}}{n}e^{-t}+1-\frac{\widetilde{a}}{n}\bigr)\bigl(\frac{\widetilde{b}}{n}e^{t}+1-\frac{\widetilde{b}}{n}\bigr)\Bigr)^{\beta n}e^{t\theta}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\sqrt{\frac{\widetilde{a}\,\widetilde{b}}{n^{2}}}+\sqrt{\bigl(1-\frac{\widetilde{a}}{n}\bigr)\bigl(1-\frac{\widetilde{b}}{n}\bigr)}\Bigr)^{2\beta n}e^{t\theta}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Bigl(1-\frac{\bigl(\sqrt{\widetilde{a}}-\sqrt{\widetilde{b}}\bigr)^{2}}{2n}\Bigr)^{2\beta n}\exp\Bigl(\frac{\theta}{2}\log R(\widetilde{a}/n,\widetilde{b}/n)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\Bigl(-\beta\,\widetilde{C}+\frac{\theta}{2}\log R(\widetilde{a}/n,\widetilde{b}/n)\Bigr)\kern 5.0pt.

∎

Theorem C.5
.

Fix a level
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor\}
and let
\beta_{i}\coloneqq 2^{-i}
(with
\alpha_{i}=1/k
).
Let
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b},\widetilde{C}
and
R(\cdot,\cdot)
be as in Theorem
C.4
, evaluated at this
(\alpha,\beta)
.
Then for every
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\ProbOp[\bm{X}\leqslant\theta]\leqslant\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{(\log 2)^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{d\,\varepsilon^{2}}{\beta_{i}\,k^{2}}+\frac{\theta}{2}\log R(\widetilde{a}/n,\widetilde{b}/n)}}\right).

Proof of Theorem
C.5
.

By Theorem
C.4
(applied with
(\beta,\alpha)=(\beta_{i},\alpha_{i})
),

\ProbOp[\bm{X}\leqslant\theta]\leqslant\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\,\beta_{i}\,\widetilde{C}_{i}+\frac{\theta}{2}\log R(\widetilde{a}/n,\widetilde{b}/n)}}\right).

It remains to lower bound
\beta_{i}\widetilde{C}_{i}
.
Starting from the definition,

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\sqrt{\widetilde{a}}-\sqrt{\widetilde{b}}\,\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Bigl(\frac{a}{b}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{i}/2}-1}}\right)^{2},

where
\gamma_{i}=\alpha_{i}/\beta_{i}
. Writing
r\coloneqq a/b>1
, we have

r^{u}-1=e^{u\log r}-1\geqslant u\log r\quad\text{for all }u>0,

so with
u=\gamma_{i}/2
,

\Bigl(\frac{a}{b}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{i}/2}-1\;\geqslant\;\frac{\gamma_{i}}{2}\,\log\frac{a}{b}.

Hence

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant b\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\gamma_{i}}{2}\,\log\frac{a}{b}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=b\,\frac{\gamma_{i}^{2}}{4}\,\log^{2}\frac{a}{b}.

Multiplying by
\beta_{i}
and using
\gamma_{i}=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\beta_{i}}
and
\alpha_{i}=\frac{1}{k}
, we get

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\,\widetilde{C}_{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant\beta_{i}\,\frac{b\,\gamma_{i}^{2}}{4}\,\log^{2}\frac{a}{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{b}{4}\cdot\frac{\alpha_{i}^{2}}{\beta_{i}}\,\log^{2}\frac{a}{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{b}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{\beta_{i}\,k^{2}}\,\log^{2}\frac{a}{b}.

Recall
a=(1+(1-\frac{1}{k})\varepsilon)d
and
b=(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k})d
, so

\frac{a}{b}=\frac{1+(1-\frac{1}{k})\varepsilon}{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k}},\qquad\frac{b}{d}=1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k},

and therefore

\displaystyle\beta_{i}\,\widetilde{C}_{i}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{d}{4\,\beta_{i}\,k^{2}}\,\Bigl(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\Bigr)\,\log^{2}\frac{1+(1-\frac{1}{k})\varepsilon}{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k}}.

Define

H(\varepsilon,k)\coloneqq\frac{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\,\log^{2}\frac{1+(1-\frac{1}{k})\varepsilon}{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{k}},\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,1],\ k\geqslant 2.

A direct computation shows that
H(\varepsilon,k)
is decreasing in
k
, so

\inf_{k\geqslant 2}H(\varepsilon,k)=\lim_{k\to\infty}H(\varepsilon,k)=\frac{\log^{2}(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

Moreover,
\log(1+\varepsilon)/\varepsilon
is decreasing on
(0,1]
, hence

\frac{\log^{2}(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\geqslant\log^{2}2\quad\text{for all }\varepsilon\in(0,1].

Thus
H(\varepsilon,k)\geqslant\log^{2}2
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
k\geqslant 2
, and we obtain

\beta_{i}\,\widetilde{C}_{i}\geqslant\frac{(\log 2)^{2}}{4}\,\frac{d\,\varepsilon^{2}}{\beta_{i}\,k^{2}}.

Substituting this into the Theorem
C.4
inequality yields

\ProbOp[\bm{X}\leqslant\theta]\leqslant\exp\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-\frac{(\log 2)^{2}}{4}\,\frac{d\,\varepsilon^{2}}{\beta_{i}\,k^{2}}+\frac{\theta}{2}\log R(\widetilde{a}/n,\widetilde{b}/n)}}\right),

as claimed.
∎

C.3
Voting lower bound

In this section, we will leverage the results of
Section

C.2
to prove the global voting concentration theorems
Theorem

4.3
and
Theorem

4.5
.

Theorem C.6

(Claim 6.2 in
[
38
]
)
.

Let
n,n_{1},n_{2}
be parameters with
n_{1},n_{2}\leqslant 10^{-6}n
.
Let
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random matrix whose entries are independent,
have mean
0
, variance at most
\sigma^{2}
for some
\sigma\geqslant 20/\sqrt{n}
,
and are almost surely bounded in
[-1,1]
.
Then, with probability at least

1-\exp\Bigl(-8\,(n_{1}+n_{2})\,\log\bigl(\frac{n}{n_{1}}+\frac{n}{n_{2}}\bigr)\Bigr),

the magnitude of the sum of the entries over any
n_{1}\times n_{2}

combinatorial subrectangle of
M
is at most
(n_{1}+n_{2})\,\sigma\,\sqrt{n}
.

As a corollary, we get the following theorem which applies for rectangles which are long and thin.

Theorem C.7
.

Let
n,n_{1},n_{2}
be parameters with
n_{1}\leqslant 10^{-6}n
and
n_{2}\geqslant 10^{-6}n
.
Let
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a random matrix whose entries are independent,
have mean
0
, variance at most
\sigma^{2}
for some
\sigma\geqslant 20/\sqrt{n}
,
and are almost surely bounded in
[-1,1]
. Then, with probability at least

1-\exp\Bigl(-8\,(n_{1}+10^{-6}n)\,\log\bigl(\tfrac{n}{n_{1}}+10^{6}\bigr)\Bigr),

the magnitude of the sum of the entries over any
n_{1}\times n_{2}

combinatorial subrectangle of
M
is at most
2\,(n_{1}+n_{2})\,\sigma\,\sqrt{n}
.

Proof.

Let us fix a set
S\subseteq[n]
of rows with
|S|=n_{1}
and define

v_{j}:=\sum_{i\in S}M_{ij},\qquad j\in[n].

Set
\delta:=10^{-6}
. By
Theorem

C.6
with parameters
(n_{1},\delta n)
, with the stated probability
we have for all
U\subseteq[n]
with
|U|=\delta n
that

\Bigl|\sum_{j\in U}v_{j}\Bigr|=\Bigl|\sum_{(i,j)\in S\times U}M_{ij}\Bigr|\leqslant(n_{1}+\delta n)\,\sigma\sqrt{n}.

We now use a simple averaging inequality: for any integers
K\geqslant L
and any vector
w
,

\sup_{|T|=K}\,\Bigl|\sum_{j\in T}w_{j}\Bigr|\leqslant\frac{K}{L}\sup_{|U|=L}\Bigl|\sum_{j\in U}w_{j}\Bigr|.

Indeed, for any fixed
T
with
|T|=K
, each
j\in T
lies in exactly
\binom{K-1}{L-1}
subsets

U\subseteq T
of size
L
, so

\sum_{j\in T}w_{j}=\binom{K-1}{L-1}^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\subseteq T\\
|U|=L\end{subarray}}\sum_{j\in U}w_{j},

and taking absolute values yields the desired result since

\binom{K}{L}/\binom{K-1}{L-1}=K/L
.

Applying this with
w=v
,
K=n_{2}
, and
L=\delta n
yields, uniformly for all
T
with
|T|=n_{2}
,

\displaystyle\Bigl|\sum_{(i,j)\in S\times T}M_{ij}\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\Bigl|\sum_{j\in T}v_{j}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{n_{2}}{\delta n}\,\sup_{|U|=\delta n}\Bigl|\sum_{j\in U}v_{j}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{n_{2}}{\delta n}\,(n_{1}+\delta n)\,\sigma\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2n_{2}\,\sigma\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\,(n_{1}+n_{2})\,\sigma\,\sqrt{n}\,.

Since
Theorem

C.6
is uniform over all
choices of
S
and
U
of the specified sizes, the bound holds simultaneously for all
n_{1}\times n_{2}

combinatorial subrectangles, as claimed.
∎

Theorem C.8
.

Fix a level
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,\lfloor\log_{2}k\rfloor\}
and set

\beta_{i}\coloneqq 2^{-i}
,
n_{i}\coloneqq 2\beta_{i}n
, and

\gamma_{i}\coloneqq\alpha_{i}/\beta_{i}=2^{i}/k
with
\alpha_{i}=1/k
.
Let
y\in\{0,\pm 1\}^{n}
be a valid level-
i
bisection labeling.
Fix parameters
t\geqslant 1
and
0<\rho<10^{-6}
, and set

\widetilde{Q}_{i}=\beta_{i}\widetilde{C}_{i}-\log(1/\rho)-3t.

Assume
a\geqslant b
,
a,b\leqslant n
, and
a\geqslant 400
. Then, with probability at least

1-e^{-t\rho n_{i}}-1/n_{i}^{3}
, for any
z
supported on
\{j:\,y_{j}\neq 0\}
with

\|z\|_{1}=\rho\,n_{i}
, we have

\langle\bar{G}\,y\odot y,\,z\rangle\geqslant 2\rho n_{i}\biggl(\frac{\widetilde{Q}_{i}}{\log\widetilde{R}_{i}}-\sqrt{a+b}\biggr).

Proof.

Imagine sampling the matrix
G
by independently drawing the entries
G_{ij}
for
i<j

and then filling in the remainder symmetrically, with
G_{ji}=G_{ij}
and
G_{ii}=0
.
If
i
and
j
are in the same community then
G_{ij}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(a/n)
;
otherwise
G_{ij}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(b/n)
.
Fix a level-
i
bisection with sides
S_{i}^{+}
and
S_{i}^{-}
of size
n_{i}/2
each, and write
\alpha=1/k
.
For a vertex
u\in S_{i}^{+}
consider the signed difference

\bm{X_{u}}=\sum_{v\in S_{i}^{+}\setminus\{u\}}G_{uv}-\sum_{v\in S_{i}^{-}}G_{uv}\kern 5.0pt.

We observe that the neighbors of
u
split into three disjoint sets:
its own community
C(u)\setminus\{u\}
of size
\alpha n-1
where edges have bias
a/n
;
the remaining
(\beta_{i}-\alpha)n
vertices on the same side where edges have bias
b/n
;
and the
\beta_{i}n
vertices on the opposite side where edges have bias
b/n
.
Independence across disjoint unordered pairs implies that these three contributions are independent binomials,
hence

\bm{X_{u}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathrm{Binom}(\alpha n-1,a/n)+\mathrm{Binom}\bigl((\beta_{i}-\alpha)n,b/n\bigr)-\mathrm{Binom}(\beta_{i}n,b/n)\kern 5.0pt.

We note that since
\langle Gy\odot y,z\rangle
is linear in
z
and only coordinates with
y_{j}\neq 0
contribute,
it suffices to take
